Śāriputra! That elder considers: “I have strength in body and hands. I could carry them out of the house in garments or upon a table.”

He further considers: “This house has only a single gate, and it is narrow. The children are young and foolish, possessing no understanding. Attached to their play places, they might fall and be burned by the fire. I must speak to them of terrifying matters: this house is already burning; you should come out quickly, do not let yourselves be burned and harmed by the fire.”

Having had this thought, just as he considered, he fully warns the children: “All of you, come out quickly!” Although the father pities them and uses good words to coax and instruct them, the children are joyfully attached to their games and are unwilling to believe and accept. Not startled and not afraid, they have no mind to emerge; neither do they know what fire is, what the house is, or what loss means. They merely run about playing to the east and west, looking back at their father.

Then the elder has this thought: “This house is already being burned by a great fire; if I and my children do not emerge in time, we will certainly be incinerated. I must now devise an expedient means to enable the children to escape this harm.”

The father knows what each child originally favored among various precious playthings and rare curiosities, things their minds would certainly delight in. He addresses them: “The playthings you cherish are rare and difficult to find; if you do not take them, you will certainly grieve and regret it later. Such things as sheep carts, deer carts, and ox carts are now outside the gate, ready for you to play with! Come out of this burning house quickly; whatever you desire, I will give to all of you!”

Then the children, hearing their father speak of the precious playthings that suited their wishes, were each emboldened in heart. Pushing and jostling one another, competing in running, they scrambled to escape the burning house.

At that time, the elder, seeing his children safely emerge, all sitting in the open clearing at the crossroads without further obstruction, felt his heart at ease, rejoicing and leaping.

Then the children each addressed their father: “Father, the playthings you promised earlier—the sheep carts, deer carts, and ox carts—pray bestow them upon us in due time!”

Śāriputra! At that time, the elder bestowed upon each of his children a great cart. The cart was lofty and broad, adorned with many treasures, surrounded by railings, with bells hanging on all four sides. Above it was pitched a canopy, also adorned with rare and precious jewels; jewel cords were intertwined, hanging with garlands of flowers, spread with layers of fine mats, and furnished with crimson pillows.

It was yoked to a white ox of plump, clean hide, handsome form, and great muscular power, walking evenly and paced as swift as the wind, with many servants attending and guarding it.

For what reason? This great elder had immeasurable wealth, his various storehouses completely overflowing. He thought: “My riches are limitless; I should not give inferior small carts to my children. Now these youths are all my sons, loved without partiality. Having such great carts of the seven treasures, immeasurable in number, I should give them equally to each with an even mind, without making distinctions. For what reason? Even if I supplied an entire country with these possessions, they would not be depleted, how much less my own children!”

At that time, the children each rode the great carts, attaining what they had never had before, surpassing their original expectations.

Śāriputra! What do you think: when this elder gave the precious great carts equally to his children, was he guilty of falsehood or not?

Śāriputra said: No, World-Honored One! This elder merely enabled his children to escape the calamity of fire and preserved their physical lives; he was not guilty of falsehood.

Why is this? If their lives were preserved, they already gained the playthings, how much more so being rescued from that burning house through expedient means! World-Honored One! Even if this elder had not given them even the smallest cart, he still would not be guilty of falsehood.

Why is this? Because this elder originally intended: “I will use expedient means to cause my children to escape.” For this reason, there is no falsehood. How much more so when the elder, knowing his wealth was limitless and wishing to benefit his children, gave them great carts equally!

The Buddha addressed Śāriputra: Excellent, excellent! It is just as you have said. Śāriputra! The Tathāgata is also like this; he is the father of all the world. Regarding all terrors, decline, afflictions, worries, and the darkness of ignorance, he has exhausted them without remainder. He has fully accomplished immeasurable knowledge and vision, powers, and fearlessnesses, possessing great spiritual power and wisdom power, fully endowed with expedient means and the pāramitā of wisdom, of great love and great compassion, constantly unwearied, ever seeking wholesome deeds to benefit all.

He is born into the decaying burning house of the three realms to liberate wandering beings from the fires of birth, old age, sickness, death, sorrow, grief, suffering, affliction, foolishness, darkness, and the three poisons, teaching and transforming them to attain anuttarasamyaksambodhi.

He sees wandering beings burned and boiled by birth, old age, sickness, death, sorrow, grief, suffering, and affliction; and because of the five desires and material gain, they undergo various sufferings. Furthermore, because of greedy attachment and seeking, they presently undergo many sufferings, and later undergo the sufferings of hells, animals, and hungry ghosts. Even if born in the heavens or amidst humans, they experience poverty, destitution, the suffering of parting from what is loved, and the suffering of encountering what is hated—various sufferings such as these.

Wandering beings are submerged in their midst, joyfully playing, unaware and uncomprehending, not startled and not afraid, giving rise to no aversion, not seeking liberation. Running east and west within the burning house of the three realms, although encountering great suffering, they do not consider it an affliction.

Śāriputra! The Buddha, having seen this, had this thought: “I am the father of wandering beings; I should rescue them from their sufferings and tribulations, and give them the joy of immeasurable, boundless Buddha wisdom with which to play.”

Śāriputra! The Tathāgata further thought: “If I merely use spiritual power and wisdom power, abandoning expedient means, and praise for wandering beings the Tathāgata’s knowledge and vision, powers, and fearlessnesses, wandering beings will not be able to attain liberation by this.

For what reason? These wandering beings have not yet escaped birth, old age, sickness, death, sorrow, grief, suffering, and affliction, and are burned by the burning house of the three realms; how could they understand the Buddha’s wisdom?”

Śāriputra! Just as that elder, although having strength in body and hands, did not use it, but merely through earnest expedient means diligently rescued his children from the disaster of the burning house, and afterward gave each of them precious great carts:

The Tathāgata is also like this. Although possessing powers and fearlessnesses, he does not use them, but merely through wisdom and expedient means rescues wandering beings from the burning house of the three realms. He expounds for them the three vehicles—Śrāvaka, Pratyekabuddha, and Buddha Vehicle—saying:

“You must not delight in dwelling within the burning house of the three realms; do not crave crude and base sights, sounds, scents, tastes, and tangibles! If you greedily attach to them and give rise to craving, you will be burned. Come out of the three realms quickly, and you will attain the three vehicles: Śrāvaka, Pratyekabuddha, and Buddha Vehicle. I now guarantee this matter for you; it will never be false. You need only cultivate diligence and vigor.”

The Tathāgata, through this expedient means, coaxes and guides wandering beings forward, further saying: “You should know that these three vehicle teachings are all praised by the sages, sovereign and unattached, free of dependence or seeking. Riding these three vehicles, by uncontaminated faculties, powers, factors of awakening, paths, dhyāna meditations, liberations, and samādhis, you will amuse yourselves and attain immeasurable peaceful security and joy.”

Śāriputra! If there are wandering beings who internally possess the nature of wisdom, who hear the Dharma from the Buddha, the World-Honored One, believe and accept it, and with earnest vigor wish to quickly emerge from the three realms, seeking nirvāṇa for themselves—this is named the Śrāvaka Vehicle, like those children who, seeking sheep carts, came out of the burning house.

If there are wandering beings who hear the Dharma from the Buddha, the World-Honored One, believe and accept it, with earnest vigor seek spontaneous wisdom, delight in solitary tranquility, and deeply understand the causal conditions of all dharmas—this is named the Pratyekabuddha Vehicle, like those children who, seeking deer carts, came out of the burning house.

If there are wandering beings who hear the Dharma from the Buddha, the World-Honored One, believe and accept it, diligently cultivate vigor, seeking all-knowledge, Buddha-knowledge, spontaneous knowledge, unguided knowledge, the Tathāgata’s knowledge and vision, powers, and fearlessnesses, pitying and bringing joy to immeasurable wandering beings, benefiting devas and humans, and liberating all—this is named the Great Vehicle. Because Bodhisattvas seek this vehicle, they are named Mahāsattvas, like those children who, seeking ox carts, came out of the burning house.

Śāriputra! Just as that elder, seeing his children safely emerge from the burning house and reach a place of fearlessness, reflected that his wealth was immeasurable, and bestowed great carts equally upon his children:

The Tathāgata is also like this. Being the father of all wandering beings, when he sees immeasurable hundreds of thousands of millions of wandering beings emerge through the Buddha’s teaching gate from the suffering of the three realms, from the perilous path of terror, attaining the joy of nirvāṇa:

The Tathāgata then has this thought: “I possess the treasuries of all Buddha-dharmas—immeasurable, boundless wisdom, powers, and fearlessnesses. These wandering beings are all my children. I will give them the Great Vehicle equally; I will not allow anyone to attain extinction alone, but will liberate them all with the Tathāgata’s extinction.”

To all these wandering beings who escape the three realms, he gives the playthings of the Buddhas’ dhyāna meditations, liberations, and the like, all of one mark and one kind, praised by the sages, able to generate pure, subtle, foremost joy.

Śāriputra! Just as that elder initially coaxed his children with three carts, and afterward gave them only great carts adorned with treasures, foremost in safety; yet that elder had no fault of falsehood:

The Tathāgata is also like this, without falsehood. First he speaks of three vehicles to guide wandering beings, and afterward liberates them solely by the Great Vehicle.

Why is this? The Tathāgata possesses the treasuries of immeasurable wisdom, powers, and fearlessnesses, able to bestow the Dharma of the Great Vehicle upon all wandering beings, though not all are able to receive it.

Śāriputra! By this causal condition, you should know that by the power of expedient means, the Buddhas distinguish and speak of three within the One Buddha Vehicle.

The Buddha, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke gāthās:

Suppose an elder

Has a great mansion;

That mansion is long decayed

And extremely dilapidated.

The halls are high and perilous,

The bases of pillars shattered and rotted,

Beams and ridgepoles slanting,

Foundations and steps collapsing,

Walls crumbling and cracking,

Plaster peeling away,

Thatch roofs falling in disorder,

Rafters slipping out of joint,

Enclosures crooked and distorted,

Filth filling it all around.

Five hundred persons

Dwell inside it.

Kites, owls, eagles, vultures,

Crows, magpies, pigeons, doves,

Vipers, snakes, adders, scorpions,

Centipedes, millipedes,

Geckos, multipedes,

Weasels, wildcats, and mice,

All evil vermin,

Scampering crosswise in confusion.

Feces and urine stench,

Impure things overflowing,

Dung beetles and insects

Gathering upon them.

Foxes, wolves, and jackals,

Chewing and trampling,

Gnawing on corpses,

Bones and flesh scattered about.

Thereupon packs of dogs

Compete to grab and snatch,

Starved, emaciated, and alarmed,

Seeking food everywhere.

Fighting, snarling, and pulling,

Growling, snapping, and barking,

That house is terrifying,

Its altered state like this.

Everywhere there are

Sprites, goblins, specters,

Yakṣas, and evil ghosts,

Devouring human flesh.

Poisonous insects of various kinds,

And evil beasts and birds,

Brooding, giving birth,

Each hiding and protecting their young.

Yakṣas compete to come,

Struggling to seize and devour them;

Having eaten their fill,

Their evil hearts become fiercer;

The sounds of their fighting

Are exceedingly terrifying.

Kumbhāṇḍa ghosts,

Squatting on mounds of earth,

Sometimes rising above the ground

One or two feet,

Wandering back and forth,

Indulging in sport,

Seizing dogs by two feet,

Striking them till they lose their voices,

Pinning their necks with feet,

Terrifying dogs for their own amusement.

There are also ghosts,

Their bodies tall and large,

Naked, black, and emaciated,

Constantly dwelling within,

Uttering loud, harsh sounds,

Crying out in search of food.

There are also ghosts

Whose throats are like needles.

There are also ghosts

With heads like oxen,

Some devouring human flesh,

Some consuming dogs,

Their hair disheveled,

Cruel and treacherous,

Pressed by hunger and thirst,

Crying out as they run.

Yakṣas and hungry ghosts,

And evil birds and beasts,

Famished, gazing in all directions,

Peering through windows.

Perils such as these,

Terrifying beyond measure.

This decaying house

Belongs to a single man.

That man had gone out,

Not long before;

Subsequently, in the dwelling,

Suddenly a fire broke out;

On all four sides simultaneously,

Its flames flared up fiercely.

Ridgepoles, beams, rafters, and pillars,

Exploding with cracking sounds,

Shattered and falling down,

Walls collapsing.

The ghosts and spirits

Cried out loudly.

Eagles, vultures, and various birds,

Kumbhāṇḍas and the like,

Frantic and terrified,

Unable to escape.

Evil beasts and poisonous vermin

Fled into holes.

Piśāca ghosts

Also dwelled within;

Having meager merit,

Pressed by the fire,

They harmed one another,

Drinking blood and devouring flesh.

Packs of jackals

Had already died earlier;

Great evil beasts

Competed to devour them;

Foul smoke billowing,

Filled all four sides.

Centipedes and millipedes,

And poisonous snakes,

Burned by the fire,

Struggled to flee their holes;

Kumbhāṇḍa ghosts

Seized and devoured them as they appeared.

Hungry ghosts,

With fires burning on their heads,

Tormented by hunger, thirst, and heat,

Ran about in frantic distress.

That dwelling was like this,

Exceedingly terrifying;

Venomous harm and the fire disaster,

Many perils, not just one.

At that time the householder

Stood outside the gate,

Hearing someone say:

“Your children,

Earlier in play,

Entered this house;

Young and foolish,

They are merrily attached to sport.”

The elder, hearing this,

Startled, entered the burning house,

Seeking to rescue them in time,

Lest they be burned and harmed.

He warned the children,

Speaking of the many tribulations:

Evil ghosts and poisonous vermin,

The spread of the disastrous fire,

Various sufferings following one another,

Continuing without break;

Vipers, snakes, adders,

And yakṣas,

Kumbhāṇḍa ghosts,

Jackals, foxes, dogs,

Eagles, vultures, kites, owls,

And centipedes,

Distressed by hunger and thirst,

Exceedingly terrifying.

These sufferings are hard to endure,

How much more so the great fire!

The foolish children,

Though hearing their father’s admonition,

Still joyfully attached,

Played on without ceasing.

At that time the elder

Had this thought:

“The children being like this

Increases my grief and sorrow.

Now in this dwelling,

There is not a single thing to delight in,

Yet the children,

Submerged in play,

Do not accept my teaching,

And will be harmed by the fire.”

Thereupon he considered,

Devising expedient means,

Addressing the children:

“I have various

Rare playthings,

Wondrous, precious fine carts:

Sheep carts, deer carts,

And great ox carts,

Now outside the gate.

Come out,

And I, for your sake,

Have made these carts;

According to your desire,

You can play with them.”

The children, hearing him speak

Of such carts as these,

Immediately scrambled,

Running to come out,

Reaching the open ground,

Free of all sufferings and tribulations.

The elder, seeing his children

Escape the burning house,

Dwelling at the crossroads,

Sat on a lion seat

And rejoiced within himself:

“I am now joyful!

These children,

Raised with great difficulty,

Foolish, young, and without understanding,

Entered the perilous house.

Full of poisonous vermin,

Terrifying sprites,

Fierce flames of great fire,

Simultaneously rising on all sides;

Yet these children

Greedily delighted in play.

I have rescued them,

Enabling them to escape disaster.

Therefore, all of you,

I am now joyful.”

At that time the children,

Knowing their father was seated peacefully,

All went to where the father was,

Addressing him:

“Pray bestow upon us

The three precious carts,

As promised before:

‘If you come out,

I will give you three carts

According to your desires.’

Now is precisely the time;

Pray deign to give them to us.”

The elder was very wealthy,

With many storehouses,

Gold, silver, lapis lazuli,

Mother-of-pearl, and agate;

With these various treasures,

He made great carts,

Adorned and decorated,

Surrounded with railings,

Bells hung on all four sides,

Gold cords intertwined,

Nets of pearls

Spread above them,

Golden flower tassels

Hanging everywhere,

Variegated adornments

Surrounding them all around,

Soft silk and cotton

Serving as cushions,

Superior, fine textiles,

Worth thousands of billions,

Pure and clean white,

Covering them above.

There were great white oxen,

Fat, robust, and strong,

Of handsome form,

Yoked to the precious carts,

With many attendants

Guarding them.

With these wonderful carts,

He gifted his children equally.

The children at that time

Rejoiced and leaped,

Riding these precious carts,

Traveling in the four directions,

Playing and joyful,

Sovereign and unobstructed.

I tell you, Śāriputra:

I too am like this,

Honored Among Sages,

The Father of the World.

All wandering beings

Are my children;

Deeply attached to worldly pleasures,

They lack wise minds.

The three realms have no peace,

Just like a burning house,

Filled with many sufferings,

Exceedingly terrifying.

Constantly there are birth, old age,

Sickness, death, grief, and worry;

Fires such as these

Blaze without ceasing.

The Tathāgata has already departed

The burning house of the three realms,

Dwelling quietly in leisure,

At peace in forest wilds.

Now these three realms

All belong to me;

The wandering beings within them

Are all my children.

Yet now this place

Has many afflictions;

I alone

Am able to rescue and protect them.

Although I teach and instruct them,

They do not believe and accept,

Because of passions and defilements,

Their greedy attachment being deep.

By this expedient means,

I expound the three vehicles for them,

Causing wandering beings

To know the suffering of the three realms,

Opening, revealing, and expounding

The supramundane path.

If these children

Have determined minds,

Endowed with the three knowledges

And the six spiritual superknowledges,

Some attain Conditioned Awakening,

Or irreversible Bodhisattvahood.

You, Śāriputra!

For the sake of wandering beings,

Through this parable,

I speak of the One Buddha Vehicle.

If you are able to

Believe and accept these words,

All of you will

Attain the Buddha path.

This vehicle is subtle, sublime,

Pure, and foremost;

In all the worlds,

It is unexcelled,

Delighted in by the Buddhas.

All wandering beings

Should praise it,

Make offerings, and bow in homage.

Immeasurable thousands of billions

Of powers, liberations,

Dhyāna meditations, and wisdom,

And other dharmas of the Buddha,

Are attained through such a vehicle as this.

Causing the children,

Day, night, and throughout kalpas,

Constantly to play with it,

With Bodhisattvas

And the Śrāvaka assembly,

Riding this precious vehicle,

Directly reaching the place of awakening.

By this causal condition,

Searching carefully in the ten directions,

There are no other vehicles,

Except for the Buddha’s expedient means.

I address Śāriputra:

All of you

Are my children,

And I am your father.

Through successive kalpas,

Burned by many sufferings,

I have rescued you all,

Leading you out of the three realms.

Although I previously said:

“You have attained extinction,”

It was only ending birth and death,

Not genuine extinction;

What should now be practiced

Is solely the Buddha’s wisdom.

If there are Bodhisattvas

Amidst this assembly,

Who can single-mindedly listen

To the genuine Dharma of the Buddhas:

The Buddhas, the World-Honored Ones,

Though using expedient means,

The wandering beings they transform

Are all Bodhisattvas.

If people of small wisdom

Are deeply attached to love and desire,

For their sake,

I expound the Truth of Suffering.

Wandering beings rejoice in their hearts,

Attaining what they never had before;

The Truth of Suffering spoken by the Buddha

Is genuine, real, and without difference.

If there are wandering beings

Who do not know the root of suffering,

Deeply attached to the causes of suffering,

Unable to abandon them for an instant,

For their sake,

I expound the path through expedient means.

The cause of all sufferings

Has greedy desire as its root;

If greedy desire is extinguished,

It has no support;

Extinguishing all sufferings

Is named the Third Truth.

For the sake of the Truth of Cessation,

One cultivates the path;

Free from the bonds of suffering,

It is named attaining liberation.

From what

Has this person attained liberation?

Merely departing from falsehood

Is named liberation;

In truth they have not yet attained

Complete liberation.

The Buddha states that this person

Has not truly attained extinction.

Because this person has not attained

The unexcelled path,

I do not intend

To let them reach extinction.

I am the King of Dharma,

At ease in the Dharma;

To bring peace to wandering beings,

I appear in the world.

You, Śāriputra!

This Dharma seal of mine

Is spoken to benefit

The world;

Wherever you travel,

Do not recklessly proclaim it.

If there are those who hear it,

Rejoice and receive it on their crowns,

You should know that such persons

Are avaivartika.

If there are those who believe and accept

The Dharma of this sūtra,

These persons have already

Seen past Buddhas,

Reverently made offerings,

And also heard this Dharma.

If a person is able to

Believe what you speak,

They have seen me,

And have also seen you,

And the Bhikṣu-Saṅgha,

And the Bodhisattvas.

This Lotus Sūtra

Is spoken for those of deep wisdom.

Those of shallow understanding hearing it

Are deluded and do not comprehend.

All Śrāvakas

And Pratyekabuddhas,

In this sūtra,

Their power cannot reach it.

You, Śāriputra!

Even in this sūtra,

You enter through faith;

How much more so other Śrāvakas!

The other Śrāvakas,

Because of believing the Buddha’s words,

Follow this sūtra,

Not through the share of their own wisdom.

Furthermore, Śāriputra!

To those of arrogance, laziness,

And those calculating a self-view,

Do not speak this sūtra.

Common mortals of shallow understanding,

Deeply attached to the five desires,

Hearing it, cannot understand;

Do not speak it to them either.

If a person does not believe

And slanders this sūtra,

They sever all

Worldly Buddha-seeds.

Or if they frown

And harbor doubts,

You should listen

To the retributions of this person’s transgressions.

Whether while the Buddha is in the world

Or after his extinction,

If there are those who slander

Such a sūtra as this,

Or seeing those who read, recite,

Write, and uphold this sūtra,

Despise, hate, and envy them,

Harboring grudges:

The retributions of this person’s transgressions,

Listen to them now.

When that person’s life ends,

They will enter the Avīci Hell,

Fulfilling a full kalpa;

When the kalpa ends, they are reborn there again.

Passing through successive cycles like this

For countless kalpas,

Emerging from hell,

They will fall among animals.

If as dogs or jackals,

Their forms emaciated,

Dark, scabby, and leprous,

Harassed by people.

Furthermore, by people

Despised and hated,

Constantly afflicted by hunger and thirst,

Bones and flesh withered,

Undergoing flogging in life,

Stoned with tiles and rocks in death.

Because of severing the Buddha-seeds,

They receive such retributions for their transgressions.

If born as camels,

Or born among donkeys,

Their bodies constantly bearing heavy burdens,

Subjected to sticks and whips,

Thinking only of water and grass,

Knowing nothing else.

Because of slandering this sūtra,

They obtain transgressions like this.

Some become jackals,

Coming into villages,

Their bodies scabby and leprous,

Lacking an eye.

By young boys

Beaten and pelted,

Undergoing many sufferings,

Sometimes meeting death.

Dying from this,

They then receive the body of a python,

Its form long and large,

Five hundred yojanas,

Deaf, dull, and without feet,

Wriggling on its belly,

Devoured by small vermin,

Day and night undergoing suffering,

Having no respite.

Because of slandering this sūtra,

They obtain transgressions like this.

If they attain human birth,

Their faculties are dull and dark,

Dwarfish, ugly, crippled, lame,

Blind, deaf, and hunchbacked.

Whatever they say,

People do not believe or accept;

Their breath constantly foul,

Possessed by ghosts and sprites.

Poor and lowly,

Employed by others,

Beset with many sicknesses, emaciated,

Having none to rely upon.

Although they attach themselves to others,

Others pay them no heed;

If they obtain anything,

They quickly forget and lose it.

If they study the art of medicine,

Treating illnesses according to formulas,

They only worsen other people’s diseases,

Or even cause death.

If they fall ill themselves,

None come to rescue or treat them;

Even if taking good medicine,

It only worsens the condition.

If others rebel,

Rob, raid, or steal,

For such transgressions as these,

They wrongly incur the blame.

Such transgressors as these

Never see the Buddha,

The King of all Sages,

Expounding the Dharma to teach and transform.

Such transgressors as these

Are constantly born in difficult realms,

Mad, deaf, their minds confused,

Never hearing the Dharma.

Throughout countless kalpas

Like Ganges sands,

Born deaf and mute,

Their faculties incomplete.

Constantly dwelling in hells,

As if roaming in gardens;

In other evil destinies,

As if in their own homes;

Camels, donkeys, pigs, and dogs

Are their walking places.

Because of slandering this sūtra,

They obtain transgressions like this.

If they attain human birth,

Deaf, blind, and mute,

Poverty and all declines

Adorning themselves.

Dropsy, consumption,

Scabs, leprosy, and boils,

Such illnesses as these

Serving as their garments.

Their bodies constantly stinking,

Filthy and impure,

Deeply attached to the self-view,

Increasing their anger,

Their lust blazing,

Not discriminating even animals.

Because of slandering this sūtra,

They obtain transgressions like this.

I tell you, Śāriputra:

Those who slander this sūtra,

If I were to speak of their transgressions,

An entire kalpa would not exhaust them.

By this causal condition,

Therefore I say to you:

“Among persons lacking wisdom,

Do not speak this sūtra.”

If there are those of sharp faculties,

Whose wisdom is clear,

Who are learned and have strong memory,

Seeking the Buddha path—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

If a person has seen

Hundreds of thousands of millions of Buddhas,

Planting roots of virtue,

Their deep minds firm—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

If a person is vigorous,

Constantly cultivating the mind of compassion,

Not begrudging body or life,

You may speak it.

If a person is reverent,

Without duplicity of mind,

Free from common fools,

Dwelling alone in mountains and marshes—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

Furthermore, Śāriputra!

If you see someone

Abandoning bad friends,

Drawing close to good friends—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

If you see a Buddha-child

Upholding pure precepts,

Like a pure bright jewel,

Seeking the Great Vehicle sūtras—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

If a person is free of anger,

Upright, direct, and gentle,

Constantly pitying all,

Reverencing all Buddhas—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

Furthermore, if there are Buddha-children

Amidst the great assembly,

Who with pure minds,

Through various conditions,

Parables, and words,

Expound the Dharma without hindrance—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

If there are bhikṣus

Who, for the sake of all-knowledge,

Seek the Dharma in the four directions,

Joining palms and receiving it on their crowns,

Only delighting in receiving and upholding

The Great Vehicle scriptures,

Not even receiving

A single verse of other sūtras—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

Like a person who with utmost mind

Seeks the Buddha’s relics,

Seeking this sūtra in the same way,

Receiving it upon the crown when obtained,

That person no longer

Aspires to seek other sūtras,

Nor ever contemplates

The scriptures of non-Buddhists—

To such persons as these,

You may speak it.

I tell you, Śāriputra:

The marks of those who

Seek the Buddha path like this,

A kalpa would not exhaust describing them.

Persons such as these

Are able to believe and understand;

You should expound for them

The Sūtra of the Lotus of the Wonderful Dharma.

Capítulo IV

Fe y comprensión

Este capítulo aún está en traducción al castellano; se muestra provisoriamente en inglés.

At that time, the Venerables Subhūti, Mahākātyāyana, Mahākāśyapa, and Mahāmaudgalyāyana, hearing from the Buddha this unprecedented Dharma and seeing the World-Honored One confer upon Śāriputra the prophecy of anuttarasamyaksambodhi, gave rise to a rare mind, rejoicing and leaping.

Immediately rising from their seats, they straightened their robes, bared their right shoulders, placed their right knees on the ground, single-mindedly joined their palms, bowed reverently, gazed up at the venerable countenance, and addressed the Buddha:

We stand at the head of the Saṅgha, old and decrepit. Considering that we had already attained nirvāṇa, we felt incapable of further responsibility and no longer advanced to seek anuttarasamyaksambodhi.

In the past, when the World-Honored One expounded the Dharma for a long time, we were in our seats, our bodies weary and sluggish, contemplating only emptiness, signlessness, and aimlessness; regarding the Bodhisattva dharmas—sporting in spiritual powers, purifying Buddha lands, and perfecting wandering beings—our minds felt no delight.

For what reason? The World-Honored One caused us to emerge from the three realms and attain the realization of nirvāṇa. Furthermore, now we are old and decrepit; regarding the Buddha’s teaching and transforming Bodhisattvas in anuttarasamyaksambodhi, we did not give rise to a single thought of fond aspiration.

Now, before the Buddha, hearing the prophecy of anuttarasamyaksambodhi conferred upon a Śrāvaka, our hearts rejoice greatly, having attained what we never had before. We never thought that today we would suddenly hear this rare Dharma, deeply congratulating ourselves on gaining great wholesome benefit; immeasurable precious treasures, unsought, have arrived of themselves!

World-Honored One! We now wish to speak a parable to clarify this meaning.

Suppose there is a man who in his youth leaves his father and runs away. He dwells long in another country—for ten, twenty, or up to fifty years. As he grows older, he falls into destitution, running about in the four directions seeking food and clothing. Gradually wandering, he happens to head toward his native country.

His father had earlier sought his son without finding him, settling in a certain city. His household is exceedingly wealthy, his treasures boundless: gold, silver, lapis lazuli, coral, amber, and crystal beads, his various storehouses completely overflowing. He has many servants, ministers, assistants, officials, and citizens; elephants, horses, carriages, oxen, and sheep beyond count. His lending and trade extend throughout other countries, and merchant traders are numerous.

At that time, the poor son, wandering through villages, countries, and towns, arrives at the city where his father resides.

The father constantly thought of his son, separated from him for over fifty years, yet had never spoken of this matter to others. He merely reflected within himself, harboring regret and grief, thinking: “I am old and decrepit, possessing abundant wealth, gold, silver, and treasures, my storehouses overflowing; yet I have no son. Once I pass away, my property will scatter and be lost, having none to entrust it to.” Therefore, he earnestly recalled his son, further thinking: “If I find my son and entrust my wealth to him, I will be joyful and carefree, having no further anxiety.”

World-Honored One! At that time, the poor son, wandering as a hired laborer, happens to arrive at his father’s house, standing by the gate.

From afar, he sees his father seated upon a lion seat, his feet resting on a jeweled footstool, reverently surrounded by brāhmaṇas, kṣatriyas, and householders. Strands of pearls and necklaces worth tens of millions adorn his body; officials, citizens, and servants holding white whisks stand attendant at his left and right. Over him is a precious canopy, hanging with flower banners; scented water is sprinkled on the ground, rare flowers are scattered, and treasures are arrayed, receiving and distributing goods—such various adornments as these, his majesty and virtue extraordinarily revered.

The poor son, seeing his father possessing such great power, is immediately terrified, regretting having come there. Secretly he thinks: “This is perhaps a king or the equal of a king, not a place where I can hire out my labor to obtain goods. It would be better to go to a poor village where labor is easy to find and clothing and food readily obtained. If I remain here long, I might be oppressed and forced to work.” Having thought this, he runs away in haste.

Then the wealthy elder, seated upon the lion seat, recognizes his son at first sight. His heart rejoicing greatly, he thinks: “My wealth and storehouses now have someone to be entrusted to. I constantly thought of this son without a means to see him, and suddenly he has arrived by himself, perfectly matching my wish. Although I am old, I still cherish him.”

He immediately dispatches an attendant to pursue and bring him back. The messenger runs swiftly and seizes him. The poor son, startled and alarmed, cries out in grievance: “I have done nothing against you, why am I seized?” The messenger grips him more tightly, dragging him back by force.

Then the poor son, thinking that though blameless he has been captured and will certainly be put to death, becomes still more terrified, faints, and falls to the ground.

The father, seeing this from afar, says to the messenger: “There is no need for this man; do not bring him by force. Sprinkle cold water on his face to revive him, and speak to him no further.”

Why is this? The father knows his son’s disposition is base and inferior, knowing that his own nobility and wealth were intimidating to the son. Ascertaining that it is his son, he uses expedient means, not telling others: “This is my son.” The messenger says to him: “I now release you; go wherever you please.” The poor son rejoices, having attained what he never had before, rises from the ground, and goes to a poor village to seek food and clothing.

At that time, the elder, wishing to attract his son, devises an expedient means. He secretly dispatches two men of emaciated appearance and no impressive demeanor: “You go there and speak gently to the poor son: ‘There is a workplace here that will pay you double wages.’ If the poor son agrees, bring him here to work. If he asks: ‘What work is to be done?’ you may say: ‘You are hired to clear dung; we two will also work with you.’”

The two messengers immediately seek out the poor son, and having found him, explain the matter fully.

Then the poor son first takes his wage, and then joins them in clearing dung. His father, seeing his son, pities and wonders at him.

On another day, through a window, he sees his son’s body from afar, emaciated and haggard, covered with dung, dust, and grime, filthy and impure. He immediately removes his necklaces, fine soft upper robes, and decorative ornaments, putting on coarse, ragged, and dirty garments, covering his body with dust, holding a dung basket in his right hand, appearing unassuming.

He addresses the workers: “Work diligently, do not slacken.” By this expedient means, he is able to approach his son.

Afterward, he addresses him: “Hey, my friend! You must constantly work here, do not go elsewhere; I will increase your wages. Whatever you need—pots, utensils, rice, flour, salt, and vinegar—do not hesitate; I also have an old servant whom you can ask for whatever you need, so put your mind at ease. I am like a father to you. Have no further anxiety.

For what reason? I am old in years, while you are young and strong. When you work, you are free of deceit, sloth, anger, resentment, and grumbling; I have never seen you have these evils like the other workers. From now on, you are like my own son, flesh and blood.”

Immediately, the elder gives him a name, calling him his child. At that time, although the poor son rejoiced at this encounter, he still regarded himself as a lowly hired laborer. For this reason, throughout twenty years he was kept constantly clearing dung.

After this time passed, they came to trust each other, and he entered and exited without difficulty, though his lodging remained in the original place.

World-Honored One! Then the elder fell ill, knowing that he would die before long. He addressed the poor son: “I now possess abundant gold, silver, and treasures, my storehouses overflowing; the quantities within them, what should be received and given, you know completely. My intention is like this; you must understand this intent.

For what reason? Now between me and you, there is no difference; apply your mind carefully, do not allow anything to be lost.”

Then the poor son, receiving his instruction, took charge of all the goods, gold, silver, treasures, and storehouses, yet harbored no desire to take even a single meal’s worth. His lodging remained in the original place, and he was still unable to abandon his inferior mindset.

After a short while, the father knew his son’s mind had gradually broadened, accomplishing great resolve and despising his previous humble attitude.

Approaching death, he summoned his son and gathered his relatives, the king, ministers, kṣatriyas, and householders, all assembling together. He publicly announced:

“Gentlemen, you should know! This is my son, flesh and blood. In a certain city, he abandoned me and ran away, enduring hardship and wandering for over fifty years. His original name was such-and-such; my name was so-and-so. In the past, in my original city, I sought him with sorrow; suddenly, in this place, I encountered and found him. This is truly my son, and I am truly his father. Now all my property belongs to my son; all that was previously received and disbursed is known to this son.”

World-Honored One! At that time, the poor son, hearing his father’s words, rejoiced greatly, having attained what he never had before, thinking: “I originally had no mind to seek anything, yet now this jewel treasury has arrived spontaneously!”

World-Honored One! The wealthy elder is the Tathāgata, and we are all like Buddha-children; the Tathāgata constantly calls us his children.

World-Honored One! Because of the three sufferings, we underwent various torments within birth and death, deluded and ignorant, joyfully attached to small dharmas. Today, the World-Honored One causes us to contemplate, clearing away the dung of discursive play of all dharmas; within this, we diligently exerted vigor, attaining the single day’s wage of nirvāṇa. Having attained this, our hearts rejoiced greatly, considering ourselves satisfied, and we claimed: “Because of diligent vigor within the Buddha-Dharma, what we have attained is vast!”

Yet the World-Honored One previously knew that our minds were attached to base desires and delighted in small dharmas, so he indulged us, not distinguishing for us: “You will have a share in the Tathāgata’s treasury of knowledge and vision.”

The World-Honored One, through the power of expedient means, expounded the Tathāgata’s wisdom. We, having attained the single day’s wage of nirvāṇa from the Buddha, considered it a great gain, having no aspiration to seek this Great Vehicle. Furthermore, relying on the Tathāgata’s wisdom, we opened, revealed, and expounded it for Bodhisattvas, yet had no aspiration for it ourselves.

For what reason? The Buddha knew our minds delighted in small dharmas, and by the power of expedient means spoke in accord with us; yet we did not know that we were truly Buddha-children.

Now we finally know that the World-Honored One is not stingy with the Buddha’s wisdom. For what reason? From the past onward, we have truly been Buddha-children, yet merely delighted in small dharmas. If we had possessed the mind to delight in the great, the Buddha would have expounded the Great Vehicle Dharma for us.

In this sūtra, he speaks solely of the One Vehicle. In the past, before the Bodhisattvas, he rebuked the Śrāvakas for delighting in small dharmas; yet the Buddha was truly teaching and transforming through the Great Vehicle.

Therefore, we say that we originally had no mind to seek anything. Now the Dharma King’s great treasure has arrived spontaneously; what a Buddha-child should attain, we have completely attained!

Thereupon, Mahākāśyapa, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke gāthās:

We today,

Hearing the Buddha’s voice and teaching,

Rejoice and leap,

Attaining what we never had before.

The Buddha proclaims that Śrāvakas

Will attain Buddhahood,

The unexcelled heap of treasures,

Unsought, has arrived of itself.

Just like a child,

Young and without understanding,

Who abandons his father and runs away,

Going far to another land,

Wandering through various countries

For over fifty years.

His father, grieving and longing,

Searches in all four directions;

Having grown weary of searching,

He settles in a certain city,

Building a residence,

Amusing himself with the five desires.

His household is immensely wealthy,

Possessing much gold, silver,

Mother-of-pearl, agate,

Pearls, and lapis lazuli;

Elephants, horses, oxen, sheep,

Palanquins, and carriages;

Fields, estates, servants,

And people in great numbers.

His lending and trade

Extend throughout other countries,

Merchant traders

Are everywhere.

Thousands of billions of people

Reverently surround him;

Constantly by kings

Loved and cherished,

Ministers and noble families

All venerate him.

Through various causes,

Those coming and going are many;

His wealth and power are like this,

Possessing great influence.

Yet his years are declining,

Increasing his sorrowful thoughts of his son;

Day and night he contemplates:

The time of death approaches.

My foolish son abandoned me

For over fifty years;

The goods in my storehouses,

What will become of them?

At that time, the poor son,

Seeking clothing and food,

From village to village,

From country to country,

Sometimes finding something,

Sometimes finding nothing,

Famished and emaciated,

His body breaking out in scabs and sores.

Gradually passing along,

He reaches the city where his father dwells;

Wandering as a hired laborer,

He finally arrives at his father’s house.

At that time, the elder,

Within his gate,

Erected a great jewel canopy,

Seated upon a lion seat;

Surrounded by his attendants,

Guarded by people,

Some calculating

Gold, silver, and treasures,

Income and disbursement of wealth,

Recording receipts and ledgers.

The poor son, seeing his father

Noble, wealthy, and dignified,

Thought he was a king,

Or the equal of a king.

Startled, he wondered

Why he had come here.

Again he thought:

“If I remain long,

I may be oppressed

And forced into labor.”

Having contemplated this,

He ran away in haste,

Inquiring after a poor village,

Wishing to seek hired work.

The elder at that time,

On the lion seat,

Saw his son from afar,

Silently recognizing him.

He immediately ordered a messenger

To pursue and bring him back.

The poor son cried out in alarm,

Fainting and falling to the ground:

“This man seizes me,

I will certainly be killed;

Why did seeking clothing and food

Bring me to this place?”

The father, knowing his son

Was foolish and base,

Would not believe his words,

Would not believe he was his father.

Immediately, through expedient means,

He dispatched other men,

One-eyed, dwarfish, ugly,

Lacking impressive demeanor:

“You speak to him,

Saying you will hire him

To clear away dung and filth,

Paying him double wages.”

The poor son, hearing this,

Joyfully followed them,

Clearing away dung and filth,

Cleaning the rooms.

The elder through the window

Constantly watched his son,

Thinking his son was foolish and base,

Delighting in lowly work.

Thereupon the elder,

Wearing ragged, dirty clothes,

Holding a dung basket,

Went to where his son was,

Using expedient means to approach him,

Telling him to work diligently:

“I will increase your wages,

Give you oil for your feet,

Plentiful food and drink,

And thick, warm mats.”

With such earnest words:

“You must work diligently.”

And with gentle words:

“You are like my son.”

The elder, possessing wisdom,

Gradually allowed him to come and go;

Passing through twenty years,

He managed the household activities.

He showed him gold, silver,

Pearls, and crystal;

The incoming and outgoing of goods,

He caused him to know them all.

Yet he remained outside the gate,

Lodging in a grass hut,

Reflecting on his poverty:

“I do not possess these things.”

The father, knowing his son’s mind

Had gradually broadened,

Wished to give him his wealth.

He gathered his relatives,

Kings, ministers,

Kṣatriyas, and householders.

Amidst this great assembly,

He said: “This is my son,

Who abandoned me and went elsewhere

For fifty years.

Since I saw my son,

Twenty years have passed.

In the past, in a certain city,

I lost this son;

Traveling around searching,

I finally arrived here.

All that I possess,

Houses, property, and people,

I entrust completely to him,

To use as he pleases.”

The son, recalling his past poverty

And base disposition,

Now from his father

Greatly obtains rare treasures,

Along with dwellings

And all wealth.

He rejoiced exceedingly,

Attaining what he never had before.

The Buddha is also like this;

Knowing that we delighted in the small,

He never said:

“You will become Buddhas.”

Instead he said that we

Attained uncontaminated dharmas,

Perfecting the Small Vehicle,

Śrāvaka disciples.

The Buddha commanded us

To speak the supreme path;

Those who cultivate this

Will attain Buddhahood.

Receiving the Buddha’s teaching,

For the great Bodhisattvas,

Through various conditions,

Parables,

And many words,

We spoke the unexcelled path.

All the Buddha-children,

Hearing the Dharma from us,

Contemplated it day and night,

Diligently cultivating it.

At that time, all the Buddhas

Conferred prophecies upon them:

“You in a future age

Will attain Buddhahood.”

The secret treasury of dharmas

Of all the Buddhas,

Only for Bodhisattvas

Did they expound the genuine reality,

And not for us

Did they speak this genuine essential.

Just like that poor son

Who drew near his father,

Although knowing all the goods,

His mind did not aspire to take them.

Although we spoke

Of the treasure store of the Buddha-Dharma,

We had no aspiration ourselves;

It was also like this.

We attained internal cessation,

Considering ourselves satisfied;

Understanding only this matter,

We had no other concern.

Even if we heard of

Purifying Buddha lands

And teaching wandering beings,

We had no delight in it.

For what reason?

All dharmas

Are completely empty and tranquil,

Without arising and without ceasing,

Neither great nor small,

Uncontaminated and unconditioned.

Contemplating thus,

We did not give rise to joy.

Throughout the long night,

Toward the Buddha’s wisdom,

We had no greed and no attachment,

Having no further aspiration;

Regarding our own Dharma,

We considered it ultimate.

Throughout the long night,

Cultivating the Dharma of emptiness,

We escaped the three realms,

The afflictions of suffering,

Dwelling in our final bodies,

In nirvāṇa with remainder.

Taught and transformed by the Buddha,

Our attainment of the path was not false;

We considered that we had already

Repaid the Buddha’s kindness.

Although for

All the Buddha-children,

We expounded the Bodhisattva Dharma

To seek the Buddha path,

Toward this Dharma,

We never had aspiration or joy.

The Guide, seeing our minds,

Left us alone;

At first he did not encourage us,

Speaking of genuine benefit.

Just as the rich elder,

Knowing his son’s base disposition,

Through the power of expedient means,

Subdued his mind,

And only afterward entrusted

All his wealth to him:

The Buddha is also like this,

Displaying rare matters;

Knowing those who delight in the small,

Through the power of expedient means,

He subdues their minds,

And only then teaches the great wisdom.

We today

Have attained what we never had before;

What was not originally expected

Has now arrived of itself,

Just like that poor son

Attaining immeasurable treasures.

World-Honored One, we now

Have attained the path and its fruit,

Within uncontaminated dharmas

Attaining the pure eye.

Throughout the long night,

Upholding the Buddha’s pure precepts,

Only beginning today

Do we attain their fruit and retribution;

Within the Dharma of the Dharma King,

Long cultivating Brahma conduct,

We now attain the uncontaminated,

Unexcelled great fruit.

We now

Are genuine Śrāvakas,

Using the sound of the Buddha path

To cause all to hear.

We now

Are genuine Arhats;

In all the worlds,

Amidst devas, humans, Maras, and Brahmās,

Universally in their midst,

We are worthy of receiving offerings.

The World-Honored One’s great kindness,

Through rare matters,

Pitying, teaching, and transforming,

Benefits us;

Throughout immeasurable billions of kalpas,

Who could repay it?

Serving with hands and feet,

Bowing with the crown of the head,

All kinds of offerings,

None can repay it.

Even if bearing him on the crown,

Carrying him on both shoulders,

Throughout kalpas like Ganges sands,

Exhausting one’s heart in reverence;

Or with delicious food,

Immeasurable precious robes,

Bedding,

Various medicinal broths,

Ox-head sandalwood,

And various treasures,

Erecting stūpas and shrines,

Spreading precious robes on the ground—

Using such things as these

To make offerings

Throughout kalpas like Ganges sands,

Still one could not repay it.

The Buddhas are rare,

Immeasurable, boundless,

Inconceivable,

Endowed with great spiritual powers,

Uncontaminated and unconditioned,

Kings of all dharmas.

Able, for the sake of the base,

To endure these matters,

To common mortals who grasp marks,

Expounding according to what is appropriate.

The Buddhas, regarding dharmas,

Attain supreme mastery;

Knowing the various desires,

Joys, and mental capacities

Of all wandering beings,

According to what they can bear,

Through immeasurable parables,

They expound the Dharma for them.

Accordant with wandering beings’

Wholesome roots of past lives,

Knowing who have ripened

And who have not yet ripened,

Pondering variously,

Distinguishing and knowing them,

Within the One Vehicle path,

They expound three according to what is appropriate.

← Capítulo anteriorCapítulo siguiente →

Capítulo V

La parábola de las hierbas medicinales

Este capítulo aún está en traducción al castellano; se muestra provisoriamente en inglés.

At that time, the World-Honored One addressed Mahākāśyapa and the other great disciples: “Excellent, excellent! Kāśyapa has skillfully spoken of the genuine virtues and merits of the Tathāgata. Truly it is as you have said: the Tathāgata furthermore possesses immeasurable, boundless incalculable virtues and merits; even if you were to speak of them for immeasurable billions of kalpas, you could not exhaust them.

Kāśyapa! You should know that the Tathāgata is the King of all dharmas; whatever he speaks is not false. Regarding all dharmas, he expounds them with wisdom and expedient means; the Dharma he expounds all reaches the ground of all-knowledge. The Tathāgata observes and knows the final destination of all dharmas, and also knows the movements deep within the hearts of all wandering beings, penetrating without obstruction. Furthermore, he fully and clearly understands all dharmas, revealing all wisdom to wandering beings.

Kāśyapa! Suppose in a great three-thousand-fold worlds, the mountains, rivers, valleys, and earth give birth to plants, trees, thickets, forests, and various medicinal herbs, of many varieties, with differing names and forms. A dense cloud spreads wide, completely covering the great three-thousand-fold worlds, raining down equally all at once, its moisture pervading everywhere. The plants, trees, thickets, forests, and medicinal herbs—those with small roots, small stems, small branches, and small leaves; those with medium roots, medium stems, medium branches, and medium leaves; those with large roots, large stems, large branches, and large leaves; trees great and small, according to whether superior, middling, or inferior—each receives a share. Rained upon by a single cloud, each grows according to its nature, blossoming with flowers and bearing fruit. Although born of the same earth and moistened by the same rain, yet the various grasses and trees have differences.

Kāśyapa! You should know that the Tathāgata is also like this. Appearing in the world like the rising of a great cloud, with a great voice he universally reaches the devas, humans, and asuras of the world, just as that great cloud completely covers the great three-thousand-fold worlds. Amidst the great assembly, he proclaims these words: ‘I am the Tathāgata, Arhat, Samyaksambuddha, Vidyācaraṇasampanna, Sugata, Lokavid, Anuttara, Puruṣadamyasārathi, Śāstā Devamanuṣyāṇām, Buddha, Bhagavān. Those who are not yet liberated I cause to be liberated; those who are not yet awakened I cause to be awakened; those who are not yet at peace I cause to be at peace; those who have not yet attained nirvāṇa I cause to attain nirvāṇa. The present world and future worlds, I know them as they truly are. I am the all-knowing one, the all-seeing one, the knower of the path, the opener of the path, the proclaimer of the path. You assemblies of devas, humans, and asuras should all come here to hear the Dharma.’

At that time, countless thousands of millions of varieties of wandering beings come to the Buddha’s abode to hear the Dharma. The Tathāgata then observes whether the faculties of these wandering beings are sharp or dull, whether they are vigorous or negligent, and expounds the Dharma for them according to what they can bear, in various immeasurable ways, causing them all to rejoice and quickly attain wholesome benefits.

These wandering beings, having heard this Dharma, are at peace in this present life and reborn in good destinies in future lives, experiencing joy through the path and also hearing the Dharma. Having heard the Dharma, they are free from obstacles; amidst all dharmas, according to their capacity, they gradually enter the path. Just like that great cloud raining upon all plants, trees, thickets, forests, and medicinal herbs, according to their nature, they fully receive moisture and each grows.

The Dharma expounded by the Tathāgata is of one mark and one flavor—that is to say: the mark of liberation, the mark of detachment, and the mark of cessation, ultimately reaching all-knowledge. If there are wandering beings who hear the Tathāgata’s Dharma, whether upholding, reading, reciting, or practicing it as taught, the virtues and merits they attain they do not perceive or know themselves.

For what reason? Only the Tathāgata knows the types, marks, substances, and natures of these wandering beings: what they are mindful of, what they contemplate, what they cultivate; how they are mindful, how they contemplate, how they cultivate; with what dharma they are mindful, with what dharma they contemplate, with what dharma they cultivate; and by what dharma they attain what dharma. Wandering beings dwell upon various levels, and only the Tathāgata sees them as they truly are, clearly and without hindrance. Just as those plants, trees, thickets, forests, and medicinal herbs do not themselves know whether their nature is superior, middling, or inferior.

The Tathāgata knows this Dharma of one mark and one flavor—namely, the mark of liberation, the mark of detachment, and the mark of cessation—the mark of ultimate nirvāṇa, constantly tranquil and extinguished, returning ultimately to emptiness. The Buddha, having known this, observes the desires in the hearts of wandering beings and protects them; therefore, he does not immediately expound all-knowledge for them.

All of you, Kāśyapa, are exceedingly rare, able to know that the Tathāgata expounds the Dharma according to what is appropriate, able to believe and accept it. For what reason? The Dharma expounded by the Buddhas, the World-Honored Ones, according to what is appropriate, is difficult to understand and difficult to know.”

Thereupon, the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke gāthās:

The Dharma King who shatters existence,

Appears within the world;

According to the desires of wandering beings,

He expounds the Dharma in various ways.

The Tathāgata is revered and honored,

His wisdom profound and far-reaching;

Long silent on this essential,

He did not hasten to speak it.

If those with wisdom hear it,

They can believe and understand;

Those without wisdom doubt and regret,

Thereby losing it forever.

Therefore, Kāśyapa!

According to their capacities he speaks for them,

By various conditions,

Causing them to attain right view.

Kāśyapa, you should know!

Just like a great cloud,

Arising within the world,

Universally covering all;

The cloud of wisdom contains moisture,

Lightning flashes gleam and shine,

Thunder rumbles afar,

Causing the multitude to rejoice.

The light of the sun is veiled,

Coolness descends upon the earth,

Dense vapor hangs and spreads,

As if it could be gathered in hand.

Its rain falls equally and universally,

Pouring down from the four quarters together,

Streaming down immeasurably,

Saturating all the land.

Mountains, rivers, perilous ravines,

And deep recesses where there grow

Plants, trees, and medicinal herbs,

Trees both large and small,

The sprouts of the hundred grains,

Sugar cane and grapes,

Moistened by the rain,

All without exception are enriched and filled;

The dry ground is universally soaked,

Herbs and trees flourish together.

What pours forth from that cloud

Is water of a single flavor;

Grasses, trees, thickets, and forests

Receive moisture according to their portion.

All the various trees,

Superior, middling, and inferior,

According to their size,

Each attains growth;

Roots, stems, branches, and leaves,

The luster and color of flowers and fruits,

Reached by the single rain,

All become fresh and lustrous.

According to their substance and marks,

The size of their nature and capacity,

That which moistens them is one,

Yet each flourishes distinctly.

The Buddha is also like this,

Appearing within the world,

Just like a great cloud,

Universally covering all.

Having appeared in the world,

For the sake of wandering beings,

He distinguishes and expounds

The reality of all dharmas.

The Great Sage, the World-Honored One,

Amidst devas, humans,

And all the assemblies,

Proclaims these words:

“I am the Tathāgata,

The Honored Among Two-Legged Beings;

Appearing within the world,

Just like a great cloud,

Moistening all.

Withered wandering beings,

I cause them all to abandon suffering,

Attaining peaceful joy—

Worldly joy,

And the joy of nirvāṇa.

Assemblies of devas and humans,

Listen well with one mind;

You should all come here

To behold the Unexcelled Honored One.”

I am the World-Honored One,

None can equal me;

To bring peace to wandering beings,

Therefore I appear in the world.

Expounding for the great assembly

The pure Dharma of amṛta;

Its Dharma is of one flavor,

Liberation and nirvāṇa.

With a single wondrous voice,

Proclaiming this meaning,

Constantly for the Great Vehicle,

Creating causal conditions.

I observe all beings,

Universally equal,

Without this or that,

Without thoughts of love or hate.

I have no greedy attachment,

Nor any limit or obstruction;

Constantly for all,

I expound the Dharma equally.

As for a single person,

So it is for multitudes;

Constantly expounding the Dharma,

Never having any other concern.

Going, coming, sitting, or standing,

Never weary or fatigued,

Filling the world,

Like rain universally moistening.

Noble or base, high or low,

Upholding precepts or breaking precepts,

Endowed with proper deportment,

Or not endowed with it,

Right views or false views,

Sharp faculties or dull faculties,

Equally I rain down the Dharma rain,

Without weariness or indolence.

All wandering beings

Who hear my Dharma,

Receive it according to their capacity,

Dwelling upon various levels.

Whether dwelling among humans or devas,

As wheel-turning sage kings,

Or kings like Śakra and Brahmā,

These are the small medicinal herbs.

Knowing uncontaminated dharmas,

Able to attain nirvāṇa,

Giving rise to six spiritual superknowledges,

And attaining the three knowledges,

Dwelling alone in mountain forests,

Constantly practicing dhyāna meditation,

Attaining the realization of Conditioned Awakening,

These are the middling medicinal herbs.

Seeking the state of the World-Honored One:

“I will become a Buddha,”

Practicing vigor and meditation,

These are the superior medicinal herbs.

Furthermore, Buddha-children,

Whose minds are devoted to the Buddha path,

Constantly practicing love and compassion,

Knowing of themselves they will become Buddhas,

Definitive and without doubt,

These are named small trees.

Dwelling at peace in spiritual powers,

Turning the irreversible wheel,

Liberating immeasurable billions,

Hundreds of thousands of wandering beings,

Such Bodhisattvas as these

Are named great trees.

The Buddha expounds equally,

Like rain of a single flavor;

According to the nature of wandering beings,

What they receive differs,

Just as those herbs and trees

Receive their endowment differently.

The Buddha, through this parable,

Expediently opens and reveals;

With various words,

Expounding the One Dharma,

Which to the Buddha’s wisdom

Is like a single drop in the ocean.

I rain down the Dharma rain,

Filling the world;

The Dharma of a single flavor

Is cultivated according to capacity.

Like those thickets, forests,

Medicinal herbs, and trees,

According to their size,

Gradually increase in lush beauty.

The Dharma of all Buddhas

Is constantly of a single flavor,

Causing all in the world

Universally to be fulfilled;

Gradually cultivating,

All attain the fruits of the path.

Śrāvakas and Conditioned Buddhas,

Dwelling in mountain forests,

Abiding in their final bodies,

Hearing the Dharma and attaining the fruit,

Are named medicinal herbs,

Each attaining growth.

If Bodhisattvas

Possess firm wisdom,

Penetrating the three realms,

Seeking the supreme vehicle,

These are named small trees,

Attaining growth.

Again, there are those who dwell in meditation,

Attaining spiritual powers,

Hearing of the emptiness of all dharmas,

Their hearts rejoicing greatly,

Emitting countless lights,

Liberating wandering beings,

These are named great trees,

Attaining growth.

Thus, Kāśyapa!

The Dharma expounded by the Buddha

Is just like a great cloud;

With rain of a single flavor,

It moistens the flowers among humans,

Each attaining fruition.

Kāśyapa, you should know!

Through various causal conditions

And various parables,

Opening and revealing the Buddha path,

This is my expedient means;

All Buddhas are also thus.

Now for all of you,

I speak the most real matter:

“The assemblies of Śrāvakas

Have none of them attained extinction.

What you practice

Is the Bodhisattva path;

Gradually cultivating and learning,

You will all become Buddhas.”

← Capítulo anteriorCapítulo siguiente →

Capítulo VI

Otorgamiento de la profecía

Este capítulo aún está en traducción al castellano; se muestra provisoriamente en inglés.

At that time, the World-Honored One, having spoken these gāthās, addressed the great assembly, proclaiming these words: “This disciple of mine, Mahākāśyapa, in a future age will attend upon and behold three million billion Buddhas, World-Honored Ones, making offerings, revering, honoring, and praising them, widely proclaiming the immeasurable great Dharma of the Buddhas.

In his final body, he will become a Buddha named Avabhāsaprabha Tathāgata, Arhat, Samyaksambuddha, Vidyācaraṇasampanna, Sugata, Lokavid, Anuttara, Puruṣadamyasārathi, Śāstā Devamanuṣyāṇām, Buddha, Bhagavān. His land will be named Prabhāsa-kalyāṇa, and his kalpa will be named Mahāvyūha.

The Buddha’s lifespan will be twelve minor kalpas; the Correct Dharma will abide in the world for twenty minor kalpas, and the Semblance Dharma will also abide for twenty minor kalpas. His realm will be adorned, free of all filth, evil, shards, rubble, thorns, briars, feces, and urine. That land will be flat and level, without elevations, depressions, pits, ditches, or mounds. Lapis lazuli will form the ground, with rows of jewel trees; cords of gold will border the roads, strewn with jewel flowers, completely pure all around.

The Bodhisattvas of that land will be immeasurable thousands of billions, and the assemblies of Śrāvakas will also be countless. There will be no activities of Mara; although there will be Mara and the subjects of Mara, they will all protect the Buddha-Dharma.”

Thereupon, the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke gāthās:

I tell the bhikṣus:

“With the Buddha eye,

I see that this Kāśyapa,

In a future age,

After countless kalpas have passed,

Will become a Buddha.

In future lives,

He will make offerings to and attend upon

Three million billion

Buddhas, World-Honored Ones.

For the sake of the Buddha’s wisdom,

He will purely cultivate Brahma conduct.

Having made offerings to the supreme

Honored Among Two-Legged Beings,

He will cultivate

All unexcelled wisdom.

In his final body,

He will become a Buddha.

His land will be pure,

With lapis lazuli as its ground;

Many jewel trees

Lining the sides of the roads,

Gold cords bordering the paths,

Delighting those who see them.

Constantly emitting fine fragrance,

Scattering various renowned flowers,

Various rare and wondrous things

Serving as adornments.

That ground will be level and flat,

Without hills or pits.

The assemblies of Bodhisattvas

Will be beyond calculation;

Their minds gentle and subdued,

Attaining great spiritual superknowledges,

Upholding the Buddhas’

Great Vehicle scriptures.

The assemblies of Śrāvakas,

Uncontaminated in their final bodies,

Children of the Dharma King,

Will also be beyond calculation,

So that even with the divine eye,

None could count or know them.

That Buddha’s lifespan

Will be twelve minor kalpas;

The Correct Dharma will abide in the world

For twenty minor kalpas;

The Semblance Dharma will also abide

For twenty minor kalpas.

The World-Honored One Avabhāsaprabha,

His activities will be like this.”

At that time, Mahāmaudgalyāyana, Subhūti, and Mahākātyāyana were all awestruck. Single-mindedly joining their palms, they gazed up at the venerable countenance without glancing away for an instant, and together with one voice spoke gāthās:

Great Heroic, Mighty World-Honored One,

Dharma King of the Śākyas,

Out of compassion for us,

Pray bestow the Buddha’s voice.

If you know our deep hearts

And deign to bestow prophecies upon us,

It will be like sprinkling amṛta,

Removing heat and attaining coolness.

Like one coming from a famine-stricken land,

Suddenly encountering a great king’s feast,

Yet harboring doubt and fear in heart,

Not daring to eat immediately;

If receiving the king’s command,

Only then daring to eat.

We too are like this;

Constantly reflecting on the faults of the Small Vehicle,

We know not how

To attain the Buddha’s unexcelled wisdom.

Although hearing the Buddha’s voice

Stating that we will become Buddhas,

Our hearts still harbor worry and fear,

Like not yet daring to eat;

If receiving the Buddha’s prophecy,

Only then will we be happily at peace.

Great Heroic, Mighty World-Honored One,

Constantly wishing to bring peace to the world,

Pray bestow prophecies upon us,

As the hungry require permission to eat.

Then the World-Honored One, knowing the thoughts in the minds of his great disciples, addressed the bhikṣus: “This Subhūti, in a future age, will attend upon and behold three million billion inconceivable Buddhas, making offerings, revering, honoring, and praising them, constantly cultivating Brahma conduct, and fulfilling the Bodhisattva path.

In his final body, he will become a Buddha named Śaśiketu Tathāgata, Arhat, Samyaksambuddha, Vidyācaraṇasampanna, Sugata, Lokavid, Anuttara, Puruṣadamyasārathi, Śāstā Devamanuṣyāṇām, Buddha, Bhagavān. His kalpa will be named Ratnaprabhāsa, and his land will be named Ratnasaṃbhava. That land will be level and flat, with crystal as its ground, adorned with jewel trees, free of hills, pits, sand, rubble, thorns, briars, feces, and urine; jewel flowers will cover the ground, completely pure all around. The people of that land will all dwell on jewel platforms and exquisite towers.

The Śrāvaka disciples will be immeasurable and boundless, beyond calculation or parables to know. The assemblies of Bodhisattvas will be countless thousands of millions of billions of inconceivable numbers. The Buddha’s lifespan will be twelve minor kalpas; the Correct Dharma will abide in the world for twenty minor kalpas, and the Semblance Dharma will also abide for twenty minor kalpas. That Buddha will constantly dwell in open space expounding the Dharma for the multitude, liberating immeasurable Bodhisattvas and Śrāvaka assemblies.”

Thereupon, the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke gāthās:

Assemblies of bhikṣus,

I now address you all;

You should all single-mindedly

Listen to what I speak:

My great disciple,

Subhūti,

Will become a Buddha,

Named Śaśiketu.

He will make offerings to countless

Tens of billions of Buddhas;

Following the practices of the Buddhas,

Gradually fulfilling the Great Path.

In his final body, he will attain

The thirty-two marks,

Upright, handsome, and subtle,

Just like a jewel mountain.

That Buddha land

Will be foremost in purity and adornment;

Wandering beings who behold it

Will all love and delight in it;

The Buddha within it

Will liberate immeasurable multitudes.

Within that Buddha’s Dharma,

There will be many Bodhisattvas,

All of sharp faculties,

Turning the irreversible wheel;

That land will constantly

Be adorned with Bodhisattvas.

The assemblies of Śrāvakas

Will be beyond reckoning,

All attaining the three knowledges,

Endowed with the six spiritual superknowledges,

Dwelling in the eight liberations,

Possessing great majesty and virtue.

When that Buddha expounds the Dharma,

He will display immeasurable

Spiritual transformations,

Inconceivable.

Devas and humans,

Their numbers like Ganges sands,

Will all join palms together,

Listening to and receiving the Buddha’s words.

That Buddha’s lifespan

Will be twelve minor kalpas;

The Correct Dharma will abide in the world

For twenty minor kalpas;

The Semblance Dharma will also abide

For twenty minor kalpas.

Then the World-Honored One further addressed the assembly of bhikṣus: “I now tell you: this Mahākātyāyana in a future age, with various articles of offering, will make offerings to and serve eight hundred billion Buddhas, revering and honoring them. After the extinction of those Buddhas, he will erect stūpas and shrines for each, a thousand yojanas in height, and five hundred yojanas in length and breadth, made of the seven treasures: gold, silver, lapis lazuli, mother-of-pearl, agate, pearls, and carnelian; with flowers, necklaces, paste incenses, powdered incenses, burning incenses, silk canopies, banners, and streamers, he will make offerings to the stūpas and shrines. After this has passed, he will further make offerings to twenty thousand billion Buddhas in the same manner.

Having made offerings to these Buddhas, fulfilling the Bodhisattva path, he will become a Buddha named Jāmbūnadakāñcanaprabhāsa Tathāgata, Arhat, Samyaksambuddha, Vidyācaraṇasampanna, Sugata, Lokavid, Anuttara, Puruṣadamyasārathi, Śāstā Devamanuṣyāṇām, Buddha, Bhagavān. His land will be level and flat, with crystal as its ground, adorned with jewel trees; cords of gold will border the roads, wondrous flowers will cover the ground, completely pure all around, delighting those who see it. It will be free from the four evil destinies: hell, hungry ghost, animal, and asura destinies. There will be many devas, humans, Śrāvaka assemblies, and Bodhisattvas, immeasurable tens of billions adorning that land. The Buddha’s lifespan will be twelve minor kalpas; the Correct Dharma will abide in the world for twenty minor kalpas, and the Semblance Dharma will also abide for twenty minor kalpas.”

Thereupon, the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke gāthās:

Assemblies of bhikṣus,

All listen with one mind;

As I have spoken,

It is genuine, real, and without difference.

This Kātyāyana,

With various

Wonderful articles of offering,

Will make offerings to the Buddhas.

After the extinction of the Buddhas,

Erecting seven-jeweled stūpas,

And with flowers and incense,

Making offerings to the relics.

In his final body,

Attaining the Buddha’s wisdom,

He will accomplish equal and right awakening.

His land will be pure,

Liberating immeasurable

Tens of billions of wandering beings,

Universally by the ten directions

Receiving offerings.

The light of the Buddha

None will be able to surpass.

That Buddha will be named

Jāmbūnadakāñcana.

Bodhisattvas and Śrāvakas,

Severing all existence,

Immeasurable and countless,

Will adorn his land.

Then the World-Honored One further addressed the great assembly: “I now tell you: this Mahāmaudgalyāyana will, with various articles of offering, make offerings to eight thousand Buddhas, revering and honoring them. After the extinction of those Buddhas, he will erect stūpas and shrines for each, a thousand yojanas in height, and five hundred yojanas in length and breadth, made of the seven treasures: gold, silver, lapis lazuli, mother-of-pearl, agate, pearls, and carnelian; with flowers, necklaces, paste incenses, powdered incenses, burning incenses, silk canopies, banners, and streamers, he will make offerings. After this has passed, he will further make offerings to two million billion Buddhas in the same manner.

He will become a Buddha named Tamālapattracandana-gandha Tathāgata, Arhat, Samyaksambuddha, Vidyācaraṇasampanna, Sugata, Lokavid, Anuttara, Puruṣadamyasārathi, Śāstā Devamanuṣyāṇām, Buddha, Bhagavān. His kalpa will be named Ratipūrṇa, and his land will be named Manobhirāma. That land will be level and flat, with crystal as its ground, adorned with jewel trees, scattered with pearl flowers, completely pure all around, delighting those who see it. There will be many devas, humans, Bodhisattvas, and Śrāvakas, their numbers immeasurable. The Buddha’s lifespan will be twenty-four minor kalpas; the Correct Dharma will abide in the world for forty minor kalpas, and the Semblance Dharma will also abide for forty minor kalpas.”

Thereupon, the World-Honored One, wishing to restate this meaning, spoke gāthās:

This disciple of mine,

Mahāmaudgalyāyana,

Having cast off this body,

Will behold eight thousand

And two million billion

Buddhas, World-Honored Ones.

For the sake of the Buddha path,

Making offerings and revering them,

In the presence of the Buddhas,

Constantly cultivating Brahma conduct,

Throughout immeasurable kalpas,

Upholding the Buddha-Dharma.

After the Buddhas’ extinction,

Erecting seven-jeweled stūpas,

With lofty golden spires,

Flowers, incense, and music,

Making offerings

To the stūpas and shrines of the Buddhas.

Gradually fulfilling

The Bodhisattva path,

In the land of Manobhirāma,

He will become a Buddha.

Named Tamālapatra

Candana-gandha.

That Buddha’s lifespan

Will be twenty-four kalpas;

Constantly for devas and humans,

He will expound the Buddha path.

Śrāvakas will be immeasurable,

Like the sands of the Ganges,

With the three knowledges and six superknowledges,

Possessing great majesty and virtue.

Bodhisattvas will be countless,

Firm in resolve and vigorous,

Regarding the Buddha’s wisdom,

All irreversible.

After the Buddha’s extinction,

The Correct Dharma will abide

For forty minor kalpas;

The Semblance Dharma will be likewise.

All my disciples,

Endowed with majesty and virtue,

Five hundred in number,

Will all receive prophecies.

In future ages,

All will become Buddhas.

Regarding the past causal conditions

Between me and you all,

I will now speak of them;

Listen well, all of you!”
